     Whole-chip read test at 7200 MT/s     
# Whole-chip read test at 7200 MT/s: old read kernel against new read kernel 
2026-11-17, all times in UTC. Machine: CRB (an Arm reference server with 128 CPUs). 120 threads on CPUs 0-119, 256 MiB per thread. 12 memory modules at 7200 MT/s. 
## {#short}Short version 
At 7200 MT/s with 12 memory modules, the new read kernel (a benchmark loop with loads only) still read 29.7 % to 31.2 % less than the old read kernel (each load is followed by an add) in 8 pairs of 6 s measurements.
For the old read kernel, no difference from the earlier 8000 MT/s runs was seen in two of the three tests (parts A and C), and in the third test (part L) it was lower by 0.3 % (nearest ends of the two ranges) to 1.3 % (medians).
The load-only loop gave higher rates than these 8000 MT/s runs in all 4 comparisons (Table 2), and the data do not show that the memory speed is the cause.  
More detail for these three sentences: 
- Sentence 1 (part C): 30.5 % to 31.2 % lower directly after the old read kernel, and 29.7 % to 31.1 % lower directly after another load-only loop (4 pairs / 2 processes each).
- Part L (sentences 2 and 3), 7200 MT/s against 8000 MT/s: n = 6 intervals / 3 processes / 3 sessions / 1 boot against 11 intervals / 7 processes / 1 boot. A boot is one run of the machine between two restarts. Old read kernel: 461.61 to 464.29 against 465.53 to 470.05 GB/s. It was lower by 1.24 GB/s at the nearest ends and by 6.27 GB/s between the medians (0.3 % and 1.3 % of the 8000 MT/s values). The data do not show the cause. Load-only loop (asmread in place of the new read kernel): 321.65 to 327.93 against 288.54 to 309.17 GB/s.
- Parts A and C (sentence 2): the tests cannot exclude a difference of about 1.2 % and 1.0 % (est.). Table 2 gives the ranges and n.
- Part C (sentences 2 and 3): two rules fixed before the run contradict each other on whether part C is compared with the 8000 MT/s runs. This page compares it. That choice was made after the numbers were seen (note under Table 2). Without part C, sentence 2 keeps parts A and L, and sentence 3 keeps 3 of its 4 comparisons.
- Sentence 3: each memory speed has only 1 boot. F72d12 (earlier run of the same boot, not part of the rerun) ran the test line of part L. Its load-only loop gave 301.90 to 302.78 GB/s (2 intervals / 1 process / no session / 1 boot). That is inside the 8000 MT/s range (288.54 to 309.17 GB/s). It is 18.87 GB/s below part L at the nearest ends. 
[DETAILS] 
[SUMMARY] Words used here Q1, Q2The two questions of the rerun. Q1: is the whole-chip read rate of the new read kernel still below that of the old read kernel at 7200 MT/s, and by how much. Q2: did the rate of either kernel differ from the earlier runs at 8000 MT/s.RerunThe 10 steps that go.sh ran at 7200 MT/s on 2026-11-17 from 22:12:09 to 22:22:18 UTC: 3 sessions of parts A, L and C, and one more part C process. Parts A and L repeat tests that ran earlier at 8000 MT/s. Part C is new. No earlier run in the results folder used one of its two argument lines. Runs of the older runner bwx timed the same three kernels in both orders inside a longer sequence of 8 kernels (note under Table 2). Control I (added after the first numbers were seen) and F72d12 (earlier run of the same boot) are not part of the rerun.CRBThe Arm reference server under test. It has 128 CPUs.Whole chip, 120 threadsThe runs use 120 threads on CPUs 0-119 only. Each thread reads its own buffer of 256 MiB from memory (1 MiB is 2^20 bytes).MT/sMillion transfers per second, the unit of memory speed.Memory moduleOne DIMM (dual in-line memory module). CRB has 12 slots for them.GB/sGigabytes per second. 1 GB is 1e9 bytes.Read kernelThe inner loop of the benchmark that reads memory. In this page "kernel" means this loop. The operating system kernel is always written "Linux kernel".Old read kernelPortable C code with 16 running sums. Each load is followed by an add. Its name is read in the binary bw_old and oldread in the runner bwx12.New read kernelNEON intrinsics (C functions that map to Arm vector instructions), loads only. Its name is read in the binary bw_new and read in bwx12.asmreadA hand-written load-only loop. It has the same 8 "ldp q" loads per 256 B as the new read kernel. "ldp q" is the Arm instruction that loads two 16-byte vector registers.Load-only loopThe new read kernel or asmread.bandwidth.c, bw_old, bw_newThe benchmark and its two binaries of 2026-11-13. bw_old was built from the old source, bw_new from the new source.bwx12, bwx11, bwxLater runner programs for the same loops. They print one row per thread. bwx12 is the runner of the investigation of 2026-11-15 into the lower read rate of the load-only loop. bwx11 and bwx are older versions.Process, interval, round, positionA process is one start of a benchmark binary. An interval is one timed measurement inside a process. bwx12 times a sequence of kernels and repeats the sequence. Each repeat is a round (counted from 0). The position is the place of a kernel in the sequence (counted from 0). In part A the script runs its 12 read, write and copy processes twice. Each run is a round (1 or 2). Round 2 runs the processes in the reverse order.RatePart A: the column aggregate_GBps that bandwidth.c prints. bwx12 and bwx: the sum over the threads of bytes / elapsed time. Unit GB/s.Interval row, thread rowsbwx12 and bwx write two kinds of row into a csv file that this page uses. An interval row starts with the letter I and holds one interval. A thread row starts with the letter T and holds one thread of one interval. The 120 thread rows of an interval follow its interval row.MedianThe middle value of a sorted list. For an even number of values it is the mean of the two middle values.Facts sheetThe file FACTS.md with its data file facts.json. It is the detailed record of this rerun. A script built it from the raw result files.R, SShort forms of two folders: R is the results folder, and S is the scratch folder of this rerun. The reference list gives both paths.Session, stepOne pass of go.sh through part A, part L and part C. go.sh ran 3 sessions and then one more part C process (T72C4). The n column calls T72C4 the extra process. A step is one part of one session, the extra part C process, or one process of control I (added after the first numbers were seen).go.sh, go2.shThe driver scripts. go.sh ran the planned rerun. go2.sh ran control I (added after the first numbers were seen).Part AThe 120-thread part of the test of 2026-11-13, repeated with the same two binaries. Each session ran 24 processes: 6 kernels (read, write, copy, asmread, asmwrite, asmcopy) x 2 binaries x 2 rounds. This page uses the 4 read processes of each session. The facts sheet holds the other 20 (its sections 3.1 and 5) [A href=#ref1][Ref1][/A].Part LOne bwx12 process that times oldread, then asmread, then three other test loops that this page does not use. The process runs this sequence twice (rounds 0 and 1).Part C, cfwd, crev, kernel orderOne bwx12 process with the sequence oldread, read, asmread (cfwd) or asmread, read, oldread (crev). Each process runs its sequence twice (rounds 0 and 1). The kernel order is the order of the kernels in this sequence.Control IThe part L line, started from an idle machine, twice. It was added after the first numbers were seen. It is not part of the plan fixed before the run.F72d12One process of the part L line. It ran at 20:02 UTC on 2026-11-17 for another test, in the same boot as the rerun and before it. It is not part of the rerun.T72A1 to T72I2The names of the result folders of the rerun (parts A, L, C) and of control I (I, added after the first numbers were seen): T72, then the letter, then a number.A-1, A-2, A-3The 8000 MT/s rows for part A, all of 2026-11-13, with the same two binaries. A-1 is the original test. A-2 is a second run with another process order. A-3 is three more processes of bw_old read.L-1, L-2, L-3, L-4The 8000 MT/s rows for parts L and C, all with bwx12. L-1 (2026-11-17) used the argument line of part L unchanged. L-2 to L-4 (2026-11-15) used 4 s measurements and counted other hardware events of the CPU. From these rows only oldread at position 0 and asmread at position 1 are used.a6456, p1nop, p2add, st1, st2, st3, thpNames of argument lines. In the rows L-1 to L-4 and in F72d12 (earlier run of the same boot, not part of the rerun) a bwx12 process carries the name of its line, and so does its csv file. A tooltip names such a process by this name. a6456 is the part L line.onebuf-ms500-1 to onebuf-ms500-3The tags that the csv file of row A-3 gives its three processes.Line, argument line, test line, spec fileThe arguments of one bwx12 process, as a spec file holds them. "The same line" means a process with the same arguments. A spec file is a text file with one argument line.VisitOne time at which the same line was run. A later visit is the same line run again at a later time.Idle startA launch at a 1-minute load average below 1.0.Whole-pass stopThe stop rule of bandwidth.c: after the minimum time (0.5 s here) each thread finishes the pass over its buffer that it is in. The threads stop at different times.Pass countPart A only: the number of buffer passes that all 120 threads completed in one interval.Pair, dropA pair is one load-only interval and one old-kernel interval of the same process and round. The drop of a pair is (1 - load-only rate / old-kernel rate) x 100, in percent. In part A the two kernels run in two processes. There the drop uses the medians of the two processes of one session and round.List of 8000 MT/s rowsThe 8000 MT/s runs with 12 modules that this page compares with: rows A-1 to A-3 and L-1 to L-4. The rules file fixed them before the run. The rules file also names rows of two older runner binaries (bwx11 and bwx). This page does not use the bwx11 rows. It shows the bwx rows in one note under Table 2 only. Two more notes under Table 2 show rows that the rules file names and that are not rows of the list: row X2d12, and three boots with 10 modules. No wording comes from them.PoolThe intervals of several rows taken together as one range.Overlap of two rangesTwo min-to-max ranges share at least one value.Distance of the nearest ends, farthest endsFor two ranges that do not overlap: the lowest value of the higher range minus the highest value of the lower range. The distance of the farthest ends is the highest value of the higher range minus the lowest value of the lower range.nThe number of measurements that a range comes from. It is written as intervals / processes / sessions / boots (rule V1 point 1). "No session" marks a row that is not part of a session of go.sh.Rules V1 to V4, F1, F2The comparison rules in baselines-and-rules.txt.Boot, boot idA boot is one run of the machine from a start to the next restart. The boot id is a random identifier that Linux creates at each start. Equal boot ids mean the same boot. The rerun ran in boot 3bfc57a1 (the first 8 characters of its boot id).MemTotalThe usable memory that Linux reports, in kB.State lineA line that go.sh or go2.sh prints with the time, the boot id, MemTotal, the load, the logins and the processes per user.Load average, loadThe number of tasks that are runnable or wait for disk input and output, averaged by Linux over the last 1, 5 and 15 minutes (file /proc/loadavg). "Load at launch" is the 1-minute value read directly before a process starts. The fourth field of the file gives the tasks that are runnable now and the number of all tasks.%CPU, process listA process list is the output of the tool ps that a run script saves before a launch. %CPU is the column pcpu of this output. It is the CPU time that a process has used, divided by the time since the process started. So it is an average over the life of the process. It is not the use at the moment of the reading.pr/ttyAMA-1A Linux kernel thread in the process lists. Its name suggests that it prints Linux kernel messages to the serial console ttyAMA. This meaning was not checked on CRB.Exit statusThe number that a process returns when it ends. 0 means success.RootAdministrator rights on the machine. They were not approved for this task.Journal, kernel logThe kernel log is the list of messages of the Linux kernel. The journal is the saved system log of one boot. It holds the kernel log.Hardware error record, corrected recordA message that the firmware passes to Linux when hardware reports an error. Linux prints it in the kernel log with the tag "[Hardware Error]". Its severity is "corrected" (the hardware repaired it) or "recoverable" (not repaired, the system continues).Module listThe output of the command dmidecode -t 17. It holds one entry "Memory Device" per memory module, with the configured speed of the module.Firmware setting, DIMM Frequency, TXREQA firmware setting is a value that the machine applies at its next start. "DIMM Frequency" sets the memory speed. TXREQ is a group of settings that were changed for another investigation.GiB2^30 bytes.est.Estimated, not measured directly. 
[/DETAILS]
## {#q1}1. Is the new read kernel still below the old one? (Q1) 
- The rerun ran parts A, L and C three times (three sessions) and then part C once more, from 22:12:09 to 22:22:18 UTC, all in boot 3bfc57a1 [A href=#ref2][Ref2][/A][A href=#ref3][Ref3][/A].
- Part A repeats the 120-thread part of the test of 2026-11-13 with the same two binaries [A href=#ref4][Ref4][/A]. With its whole-pass stop the pass count of an interval changes by whole passes, and the rate changes with it. So part A is less exact than parts L and C [A href=#ref1][Ref1][/A].
- Part C runs the new read kernel itself [A href=#ref5][Ref5][/A][A href=#ref6][Ref6][/A]. Part L runs asmread in its place [A href=#ref7][Ref7][/A]. asmread is a hand-written loop with the same loads [A href=#ref1][Ref1][/A].
- This page does not examine why the load-only loop is lower. The investigation of 2026-11-15 examined it and did not establish the cause [A href=#ref8][Ref8][/A].

Figure 1. In the 6 rows of the same test line at 7200 MT/s the blue dot (old read kernel) stays at nearly the same place, and the orange dot (load-only loop) moves. The intervals of the blue dots span 7.59 GB/s, and those of the orange dots span 26.03 GB/s. The 6 rows are part L, control I (added after the first numbers were seen) and F72d12 (earlier run of the same boot, not part of the rerun). Their ranges are 461.61 to 469.20 GB/s (old read kernel) and 301.90 to 327.93 GB/s (load-only loop). The orange dot is left of the blue dot in every row of the figure. So the load-only rate is lower in every row. 
- Each dot is the median of the intervals of its row. Point at a mark to see its values and its source file.
- For bwx12 a source names the interval row of the csv file. The rate is the sum of the 120 thread rows after it. This sum differs from the rate printed in the interval row by at most 0.02 GB/s.
- The data of both figures come from facts.json, the data file of the facts sheet [A href=#ref9][Ref9][/A].

## {#q2}2. Did a rate differ from the 8000 MT/s runs? (Q2) 
- The comparison rules and the list of 8000 MT/s rows were fixed before the run [A href=#ref26][Ref26][/A]. A rule compares the lowest-to-highest range of the 7200 MT/s intervals with that of the 8000 MT/s rows.
- Overlap: "no difference seen". The width of the 8000 MT/s range is then the size that the test cannot exclude (est.).
- No overlap: "differs between the 8000 MT/s boot and the 7200 MT/s boot".
- Each memory speed has exactly 1 boot. So a difference between the two boots is not shown to be an effect of the memory speed.

- A 7200 MT/s row with all dots left of the band, or all dots right of it, does not overlap the 8000 MT/s range.
- The bold text under a row name says where the row lies relative to the band: left of it (lower), inside it, overlapping it, or right of it (higher). A 7200 MT/s row without bold text is not compared with a band.
- Panel "Load-only loop", 0.5 s blocks: only session 1 is compared, each round against the same round (rule V3). Sessions 2 and 3 started 5 s after a 120-thread process of part C. Session 1 and the rows A-1 and A-2 did not. Their first process started at a 1-minute load average of 0.07, 1.00 and 0.64 (Table 6).
- The figure shows 139 intervals. Control I was added after the first numbers were seen. Table 2 gives n for every comparison, except the three part A session rows of the old read kernel. Each of them has 6 intervals / 2 processes / 1 session / 1 boot and is a part of the Table 2 row "Part A (bw_old read)".

 | Pool or L-1 alone: with row L-1 alone in place of the pool L-1 to L-4, 7 of the 8 rows that use the pool keep their overlap result.  | Part C: two rules of the rules file contradict each other here. Rule F2 (points 2 and 3) would show part C at 7200 MT/s only. This page follows the facts sheet (its decision (r) in section 2) and compares the two part C rows that have an 8000 MT/s row of the list [A href=#ref1][Ref1][/A][A href=#ref26][Ref26][/A]. This choice was made after the numbers were seen.
 | Row X2d12 is shown here and not used. X2d12 gave 469.36 to 470.68 GB/s for the old read kernel and 301.95 to 307.08 GB/s for asmread (2 intervals / 1 process / 1 boot per kernel). With X2d12 added to the pool L-1 to L-4, all 8 rows that use the pool keep their overlap result.
 | 
Other runner binary (bwx, not the bwx12 binary of the rerun): rule V2 (point 4) names 8000 MT/s rows (12 modules) of the older runner bwx for the comparison with part C [A href=#ref26][Ref26][/A].
- These rows differ from part C in three ways. They used the older binary bwx. The processes of the two result folders B7-* ran without the counting of hardware events of the CPU (count=0 in the csv header), and those of the two folders C3-* ran with it (count=1). 12 of the 24 processes used the runner option --start offset. Part C used bwx12 with the counting of hardware events and --start common [A href=#ref38][Ref38][/A][A href=#ref39][Ref39][/A][A href=#ref40][Ref40][/A][A href=#ref41][Ref41][/A] [A href=#ref10][Ref10][/A][A href=#ref11][Ref11][/A][A href=#ref12][Ref12][/A][A href=#ref13][Ref13][/A].
- The highest of their load-only intervals (192 intervals / 24 processes / 1 boot) was 319.11 GB/s for kernel read and 320.54 GB/s for asmread [A href=#ref38][Ref38][/A][A href=#ref39][Ref39][/A][A href=#ref40][Ref40][/A][A href=#ref41][Ref41][/A].
- Three sets of part C intervals have such bwx rows with the same kernel directly before (48 intervals / 24 processes / 1 boot per set at 8000 MT/s). Each set lies above the highest value of its bwx rows. The distances are those of the nearest ends. The set "read after oldread" (4 intervals / 2 processes / sessions 1 and 3 / 1 boot) lies 5.60 GB/s above 315.29 GB/s. The set "read after asmread" (4 intervals / 2 processes / session 2 and 1 extra process / 1 boot) lies 3.11 GB/s above 319.11 GB/s. The set "asmread after read" (4 intervals / 2 processes / sessions 1 and 3 / 1 boot) lies 0.32 GB/s above 320.54 GB/s.
- The 4 bwx processes of two other result folders ran another kernel order. They reached 324.36 GB/s (read) and 324.98 GB/s (asmread) (4 intervals / 4 processes / 1 boot per kernel) [A href=#ref42][Ref42][/A][A href=#ref43][Ref43][/A]. The read value lies inside the 7200 MT/s range of both read rows of part C (after oldread and after asmread). The asmread value lies inside the 7200 MT/s asmread range of part L.
 | 
Boots with 10 modules (rule V1, points 3 and 4) [A href=#ref26][Ref26][/A]: on 2026-11-17 three boots at 8000 MT/s found 10 modules. Each ran the argument line of part L once [A href=#ref44][Ref44][/A][A href=#ref45][Ref45][/A][A href=#ref46][Ref46][/A][A href=#ref47][Ref47][/A][A href=#ref48][Ref48][/A][A href=#ref49][Ref49][/A]. They are not rows of the list, and no wording comes from them.
- By the settings records of the results folder, two of these boots had the same firmware settings (TXREQ Min changed) [A href=#ref50][Ref50][/A][A href=#ref51][Ref51][/A]. Their asmread gave 260.37 / 262.67 GB/s and 285.57 / 286.45 GB/s (2 intervals / 1 process / 1 boot each). The two medians are 9.4 % apart (percent of the lower median).
- The three boots gave 359.56 to 362.15 GB/s for the old read kernel (6 intervals / 3 processes / 3 boots). This range is 0.7 % wide.
- Limit: by the same records the third boot had the original firmware settings [A href=#ref52][Ref52][/A]. So the three boots did not differ by the boot alone.
 | Rounding: a distance is computed from the rates before rounding. So it can differ by 0.01 GB/s from the difference of the two rounded rates. Examples: 1.73 GB/s (this table) and 39.38 GB/s (Table 4). 
### The rate of the load-only loop changed inside the one 7200 MT/s boot 
- The part L line ran in three visits of the 7200 MT/s boot (Table 3): F72d12 (earlier run of the same boot, not part of the rerun), part L, and control I (added after the first numbers were seen).
- The three asmread ranges do not overlap each other. Together they span 301.90 to 327.93 GB/s (26.03 GB/s).
- The three visits did not start from the same state. The data do not show which difference changed the rate.

## {#open}3. What the data do not show 
- Insufficient data: whether a difference of the new read kernel comes from the memory speed. Runs in several boots per speed would resolve it (needs reboots and a change of the firmware setting).
- Insufficient data: why the rate of the load-only loop changed between the three visits of the one 7200 MT/s boot (Table 3). Two measurements would resolve it. First: run the part L line alternately from an idle start and directly after part A, at least 3 times each in one boot. Second: repeat the idle-start line some hours later in the same boot, and once directly after a reboot (needs approval).
- Insufficient data: why the old read kernel was lower in part L. The first of these two measurements would resolve it.
- Insufficient data: no 8000 MT/s run of kernel read with bwx12. A run of the two part C lines at 8000 MT/s would resolve it (needs a firmware change and a reboot).
- Insufficient data: whether hardware error records occurred during the rerun. A count of the "[Hardware Error]" lines of the kernel log would resolve it (needs root, which was not approved for this task).
- Insufficient data: a direct reading of the memory speed during the rerun. A module list read during the rerun would resolve it. The command sudo dmidecode -t 17 prints this list (needs root). 
## {#state}4. Machine state and system load 
Table 5. Machine state: each fact with its proof and its limit. 
If the table does not fit the screen, scroll it sideways.
 | Fact | Proof | Limit
 | 12 memory modules during the rerun | 
- MemTotal is 395392808 kB in all 18 state lines of the two run logs [A href=#ref3][Ref3][/A][A href=#ref61][Ref61][/A].
- The record of this boot (a file written at 20:02:21 UTC) holds the same value. It also holds the kernel log line "This system has 12 DIMM sockets" and the count 12 of the lines "Configured Memory Speed" [A href=#ref62][Ref62][/A].
- The module list saved in the same boot has one line "Configured Memory Speed" for each of its 12 modules [A href=#ref63][Ref63][/A]. | 
- The record also holds the line "modules=24" [A href=#ref62][Ref62][/A]. This is not the module count. The module list holds 24 lines with the text "Size: 32 GB": 12 lines "Size" and 12 lines "Volatile Size" [A href=#ref63][Ref63][/A].
- Hypothesis, not checked: the script that wrote the record counted these 24 lines. Reading that script would confirm or reject it.
 | Memory speed 7200 MT/s during the rerun | Every state line holds the boot id 3bfc57a1-e235-4fe5-8c09-a94642f9b60b. The record of this boot, written at 20:02:21 UTC, shows 12 x "Configured Memory Speed: 7200 MT/s" [A href=#ref62][Ref62][/A]. | The speed was not read during the rerun. Reading it needs root. Assumption, not measured: the speed does not change inside one boot.
 | The rerun is complete | go.sh ran from 22:12:09 to 22:22:18 UTC. The script stops at the first exit status other than 0 [A href=#ref2][Ref2][/A]. Its log holds 10 "done" lines and the final line ALLDONE [A href=#ref3][Ref3][/A]. So 10 of 10 steps ended with exit status 0. | -

- 886 of the 1490 entries show no readable value in both lists (883 show ValueUnavailable, 3 show VStoreError).
- "On record" means the 4 files named set.txt in the results folder.
- Insufficient data: the firmware settings in the boot of the rerun, apart from the memory speed. No full list was saved in that boot. A settings list saved in that boot would resolve it (needs root).
 | The 8000 MT/s rows: one boot, 12 modules | 
- The saved kernel log of that boot runs from 2026-11-09 21:20:15 to 2026-11-17 03:44:00 UTC and holds the line "This system has 12 DIMM sockets" [A href=#ref70][Ref70][/A].
- The kernel log of the boot that started on 2026-11-17 04:17:19 UTC holds "This system has 10 DIMM sockets", and its memory size line shows 64 GiB less [A href=#ref49][Ref49][/A]. So the number in this line is not the slot count of the board. This page reads it as the number of modules that the boot found.
- Every process of the rows A-1 to A-3, of the rows L-1 to L-4 and of runner bwx was launched inside the time of this kernel log [A href=#ref9][Ref9][/A]. Row X2d12 and the three boots with 10 modules ran in later boots (notes under Table 2).

 | Corrected hardware error records in the 8000 MT/s rows |

- The data do not show that a record changed a rate.
- For the rerun the kernel log was not read (section 3).
- The rows of runner bwx (note under Table 2) were not checked for such records.
 | The lowest asmread value of the 8000 MT/s pool comes from an interval with corrected hardware error records |

- Without this one interval the pool spans 298.61 to 309.17 GB/s (10 intervals / 7 processes / 1 boot). Both times have a resolution of 1 s. 
### System load 
Table 6. 1-minute load average of Linux, read by the scripts directly before each launch [A href=#ref9][Ref9][/A]. The first launch of the rerun was at a 1-minute load average of 0.07. The other 9 steps of go.sh started at a 1-minute load average of 74.05 to 105.24, each 5 s after a 120-thread process of the rerun had ended. Hypothesis, not tested: these high values come from the rerun's own 120-thread processes. Two more facts fit it. The load value is an average over the last minute. The state lines of the go.sh log show 0 logins and processes of jhan, root and 6 system accounts only [A href=#ref3][Ref3][/A]. A sample of the load average and of the running processes, taken once per second between two steps, would confirm or reject it. 
If the table does not fit the screen, scroll it sideways.
